will be in the
(n-1)
-th level. As explained in
Appendix

D
, the reason this holds is related to the degree of the Boolean functions
f
and
f^{\prime}
, when they are expanded as a polynomial of their
n
input bits. Specifically, we may observe that the highest-degree monomials in the expansion of
f(x)
are the same as those in the expansion of
f(x\oplus m)
. Thus, when
f^{\prime}(x)
is defined as
f(x)\oplus f(x\oplus m)
, the highest-degree monomials all cancel out, leaving only monomials of a lower degree. That is, the degree of
f^{\prime}
is smaller than the degree of
f
by at least one. We use this fact to prove in general that if a Boolean function
h
has degree
d
, then
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(h)$}}\smash[t]{V(h)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(h)$}}\smash[t]{V(h)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(h)$}}\smash[t]{V(h)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(h)$}}\smash[t]{V(h)}}}\in\mathcal{C}_{d}
. In particular, since
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}
(the maximum possible degree of any function is
n
), we have that
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f^{\prime})$}}\smash[t]{V(f^{\prime})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f^{\prime})$}}\smash[t]{V(f^{\prime})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(f^{\prime})$}}\smash[t]{V(f^{\prime})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f^{\prime})$}}\smash[t]{V(f^{\prime})}}}\in\mathcal{C}_{n-1}
.

Our protocol proposes to implement the correction
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f^{\prime})$}}\smash[t]{V(f^{\prime})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f^{\prime})$}}\smash[t]{V(f^{\prime})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(f^{\prime})$}}\smash[t]{V(f^{\prime})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f^{\prime})$}}\smash[t]{V(f^{\prime})}}}
in the same fashion as
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}
: by preparing the resource state
|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(f^{\prime})$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(f^{\prime})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(f^{\prime})$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(f^{\prime})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(f^{\prime})$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(f^{\prime})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(f^{\prime})$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(f^{\prime})}}}\rangle
and teleporting as in
Eq.

10
. This will also produce a correction, associated with a Boolean function
f^{\prime\prime}
of degree
n-2
. As we iterate, we descend the Clifford hierarchy, and the degree of our correction function is reduced. Once we have performed
n
rounds of teleportation, our correction function has degree zero. If a Boolean function
h
is constant, this implies that
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(h)$}}\smash[t]{V(h)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(h)$}}\smash[t]{V(h)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(h)$}}\smash[t]{V(h)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(h)$}}\smash[t]{V(h)}}}\propto\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathbb{I}$}}\smash[t]{\mathbb{I}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathbb{I}$}}\smash[t]{\mathbb{I}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{I}$}}\smash[t]{\mathbb{I}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathbb{I}$}}\smash[t]{\mathbb{I}}}}
; thus, once we have reduced the correction function to degree zero, we may cease iterating the protocol.

Later, in
Section

4.5
, we perform a more complete analysis of the teleportation channel; for example, we quantify the error in the teleportation channel when an imperfect resource state is teleported instead of
|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(f)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(f)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(f)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(f)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(f)}}}\rangle
.

2.4
Preparing the encoded QRAM resource state

The analysis above shows how we can implement the logical
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}
gate, provided that we can adaptively prepare the resource states
|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}\rangle
, up to low error, for any particular Boolean function
g
. At first glance, this seems like a tall task. There are
2^{2^{n}}
different states that we may need to prepare. By a simple counting argument, the quantum circuit complexity of at least one of these states is at least
\Omega(2^{n}/n)
. The innovation of our protocol is to outsource this complexity to a single-purpose, faulty (and ideally passive) QRAM device, which may be able to exploit the unique structure of QRAM to implement
V(g)
cheaply, but imperfectly.

We propose a three-step procedure for preparing these states, analogous to the preparation of the
|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$T$}}\smash[t]{T}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$T$}}\smash[t]{T}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$T$}}\smash[t]{T}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$T$}}\smash[t]{T}}}\rangle
state: physical preparation, encoding, and distillation.

•

Physical preparation
: we assume that we have access to a QRAM device that can implement an approximation to
V(g)
at the physical level, as discussed in
Section

1
and
Fig.

1
. By running this device on the initial input state
|+\rangle^{\otimes n}
, we produce the physical resource state
|\Psi(g)\rangle
of
Eq.

9
. The device can be faulty. In fact, our protocol can succeed as long as the device produces states that have at least
1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
minimum fidelity with respect to
|\Psi(g)\rangle
.

•

Encoding
: The physical
n
-qubit state is not protected by a QEC code, and thus it is vulnerable to error. We immediately encode it into (an approximation of) the logical state
|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}\rangle
using some number
n^{\prime}>n
of physical qubits on our main quantum processor. This step incurs some additional logical error because encoding arbitrary states is not fully fault tolerant. However, for topological codes like the surface code, there exist effective methods for encoding a physical qubit into a logical qubit
[
49
]
. The logical error due to encoding is
O(p)
—independent of the code distance—where
p
is the physical error rate. In
Section

4.2
, we use the general results of Ref.
[
59
]
to formalize the error in this step. Since the state
|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}\rangle
is an
n
-qubit state, we expect the total logical error incurred from encoding to be
O(np)
, although for the case of general codes, we can only show
O(n\sqrt{p})
. The physical error rate must be
p=O(1/n)
or
p=O(1/n^{2})
, so that the total error from encoding remains
O(1)
, but for relevant sizes of
n
(e.g.,
n=43
already corresponds to one terabyte of QRAM), the
p=O(1/n)
condition is already met on devices that exist today.

•

Distillation
: Distillation procedures
[
52
,
51
]
for the
|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$T$}}\smash[t]{T}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$T$}}\smash[t]{T}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$T$}}\smash[t]{T}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$T$}}\smash[t]{T}}}\rangle
(or
|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathrm{CCZ}$}}\smash[t]{\mathrm{CCZ}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathrm{CCZ}$}}\smash[t]{\mathrm{CCZ}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathrm{CCZ}$}}\smash[t]{\mathrm{CCZ}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathrm{CCZ}$}}\smash[t]{\mathrm{CCZ}}}}\rangle
) state leverage the existence of QEC codes where
T
(or
\mathrm{CCZ}
) is transversal. The overhead, that is, the number of noisy copies of
|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$T$}}\smash[t]{T}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$T$}}\smash[t]{T}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$T$}}\smash[t]{T}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$T$}}\smash[t]{T}}}\rangle
needed to distill one
\varepsilon_{\rm dist}
-good copy of
|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$T$}}\smash[t]{T}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$T$}}\smash[t]{T}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$T$}}\smash[t]{T}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$T$}}\smash[t]{T}}}\rangle
is
\mathrm{polylog}(1/\varepsilon_{\rm dist})
, and this can be improved to
O(1)
overhead using high-rate codes
[
60
,
61
,
62
]
. For the
V(g)
gate, we do not know of any suitable codes that would enable this kind of approach. However, we can still distill
|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}\rangle
using state-agnostic
quantum purity amplification
methods
[
63
,
64
,
65
,
66
,
67
,
68
,
69
]
, which take many copies of an arbitrary mixed state
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}
and produce one
\varepsilon_{\rm dist}
-good copy of the pure state
|{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Xi$}}\smash[t]{\Xi}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Xi$}}\smash[t]{\Xi}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Xi$}}\smash[t]{\Xi}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Xi$}}\smash[t]{\Xi}}}}\rangle\!\langle{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Xi$}}\smash[t]{\Xi}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Xi$}}\smash[t]{\Xi}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Xi$}}\smash[t]{\Xi}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Xi$}}\smash[t]{\Xi}}}}|
, where
|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Xi$}}\smash[t]{\Xi}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Xi$}}\smash[t]{\Xi}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Xi$}}\smash[t]{\Xi}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Xi$}}\smash[t]{\Xi}}}\rangle
is the principal component (i.e., top eigenvector) of
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}
. These methods do not leverage or learn any properties of
|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Xi$}}\smash[t]{\Xi}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Xi$}}\smash[t]{\Xi}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Xi$}}\smash[t]{\Xi}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Xi$}}\smash[t]{\Xi}}}\rangle
, and it is known that the optimal overhead achievable in such settings is
\Theta(1/\varepsilon_{\rm dist})

[
68
]
. In
Section

4.4
, we discuss several specific state-agnostic approaches. We first consider the iterated swap test purification method studied in Refs.
[
66
,
70
,
67
,
69
]
, which is appealing for its simplicity. In the regime where the physical preparation and encoding steps prepare states with high (but still imperfect) fidelity, the iterated swap test approach is nearly optimal. On the other hand, as the fidelity of the undistilled input states decreases, the overhead of the iterated swap test rapidly increases, scaling exponentially in the inverse input fidelity. To alleviate this issue, we propose a new gate-efficient state-agnostic quantum purity amplification procedure based on quantum principal component analysis
[
71
,
72
]
, which achieves nearly optimal sample complexity even in the regime of low input fidelity, while still being compatible with the streaming model (i.e., where the undistilled input states are processed one at a time, rather than all at once as in the known sample-optimal protocol
[
69
]
).

To apply these state-agnostic distillation approaches within our protocol, it must be the case that the state that is output by the physical preparation and encoding processes has the ideal resource state
|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}\rangle
as its principal component. Evaluating this assertion requires specifying a noise model in our abstract QRAM device and in our main quantum processor. We suppose that our main processor is subject to circuit-level stochastic noise. For the QRAM device, the only assumption we make is that the noise is independent of the dataset, in the sense that, for dataset
g
, it enacts the
n
-qubit quantum channel
\mathcal{N}_{2}\circ\mathcal{V}(g)\circ\mathcal{N}_{1}
, where
\mathcal{N}_{1,2}
are
g
-independent noise channels, and
\mathcal{V}(g)=V(g)[\cdot]V(g)^{\dagger}

is the ideal QRAM channel. In this case, we can ensure that the principal component of the state we prepare is
|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}\rangle
by performing a
partial Clifford-twirl
of the unitary
V(g)
. This method leverages the fact that for any Clifford circuit
C
formed from
Z
,
X
,
\mathrm{CZ}
, and
\mathrm{CX}
(i.e., CNOT) gates, we have
|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}\rangle=C|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g_{C})$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g_{C})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g_{C})$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g_{C})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g_{C})$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g_{C})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g_{C})$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g_{C})}}}\rangle
for some dataset
g_{C}
; the idea is to choose a random
C
, compute the dataset
g_{C}
, query
g_{C}
with the QRAM device, and then apply
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$C$}}\smash[t]{C}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$C$}}\smash[t]{C}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$C$}}\smash[t]{C}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$C$}}\smash[t]{C}}}
fault-tolerantly to restore
|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}\rangle
. Partial Clifford twirling is not necessary under the stronger assumption that the noise in the QRAM device naturally guarantees that the ideal resource state is the principal component.

It is important that our protocol can work even when the QRAM device has low (at least inverse polynomial) fidelity. Given the engineering challenges associated with building a reliable physical QRAM device, it is much easier to imagine realizing our protocol in practice, especially as
n
grows, if the physical QRAM device need only have a small correlation with the correct output. Along these lines, another key benefit of a distillation–teleportation approach to fault-tolerant QRAM is that one always has the option to restart the preparation, encoding, and distillation procedure if an error is detected. For instance, if the physical QRAM device recognizes certain errors (e.g., photon loss), one can simply postselect on these events not occurring, improving the effective fidelity of the device from the perspective of our protocol.
