\eta^{(i)}\equiv\eta^{({\rm end},i)}:=1-p^{({\rm end},i)}_{1}\equiv 1-p^{(i)}_{1}
and
\eta^{({\rm start},i)}:=1-p^{({\rm start},i)}_{1}\equiv 1-p^{(i-1)}
.
Similarly to
Eq.

93
, by
Eqs.

87
and
88
we get for
i=1,\ldots,\ell
that

\displaystyle\eta^{(i)}=1-\frac{p^{({\rm proj},i)}_{1}}{p^{({\rm proj},i)}_{1}+\sum_{j>1}p^{({\rm proj},i)}_{j}}

\displaystyle\leq 1-\frac{p^{({\rm start},i)}_{1}(1-\epsilon_{i}-\zeta_{i})}{p^{({\rm start},i)}_{1}(1-\epsilon_{i}-\zeta_{i})+\sum_{j>1}p^{({\rm proj},i)}_{j}}

(by monotonicity of
\frac{x}{x+c}
and
Eq.

87
)

\displaystyle\leq 1-\frac{p^{({\rm start},i)}_{1}(1-\epsilon_{i}-\zeta_{i})}{p^{({\rm start},i)}_{1}(1-\epsilon_{i}-\zeta_{i})+(\epsilon_{i}+\zeta_{i})(1-p^{({\rm start},i)}_{1})+\zeta_{i}(1-\lambda_{1})}

(by
Eq.

88
)

\displaystyle=\frac{(\epsilon_{i}+\zeta_{i})\eta^{({\rm start},i)}+\zeta_{i}(1-\lambda_{1})}{(1-\eta^{({\rm start},i)})(1-\epsilon_{i}-\zeta_{i})+(\epsilon_{i}+\zeta_{i})\eta^{({\rm start},i)}+\zeta_{i}(1-\lambda_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(\epsilon_{i}+\zeta_{i})(\eta^{({\rm start},i)})+\zeta_{i}(1-\lambda_{1})}{(1-\eta^{({\rm start},i)})(1-\epsilon_{i}-\zeta_{i})}=\frac{(\epsilon_{i}+\zeta_{i})(\eta^{(i-1)})+\zeta_{i}(1-\lambda_{1})}{(1-\eta^{(i-1)})(1-\epsilon_{i}-\zeta_{i})}\,.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\eta^{(i)}\leq\frac{\eta^{(i-1)}+\zeta_{i}(1-\lambda_{1})}{4}\quad\text{ as long as }\quad\eta^{(i-1)}\leq\frac{1}{3}\text{ and }\epsilon_{i}+\zeta_{i}\leq\frac{1}{8}.

(94)

We choose
\ell:=\lceil\log_{2}(\frac{1-\gamma}{\varepsilon_{\rm dist}})\rceil
,
\zeta_{i}:=2^{-3-i}
, and
\epsilon_{i}:=2^{-4-\ell+i}
.
Since
\eta^{(0)}\leq 1-\gamma
, using
Eq.

94
and induction we see that
\eta^{(i)}\leq 2^{-i}(1-\gamma)
and therefore
\eta^{(\ell)}\leq\varepsilon_{\rm dist}
.

As
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\epsilon_{i}+\zeta_{i}\leq\frac{1}{4}
, we get by
Eq.

92
that the sequence of iterations succeeds with probability
\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}p^{({\rm succ},i)}\geq\frac{3}{4}p^{(0)}_{1}\geq\frac{1}{2}
. Therefore, the expected complexity can be bounded up to a constant factor by

\displaystyle C+\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\frac{\log_{2}^{2}(1/\epsilon_{i})}{\delta^{2}\zeta_{i}}=C+\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\frac{2^{i+3}(4+\ell-i)^{2}}{\delta^{2}}=C+\frac{2^{\ell+3}}{\delta^{2}}\sum_{i^{\prime}=0}^{\ell-1}2^{-i^{\prime}}(i^{\prime}+4)^{2}=O\left(\frac{1}{(1-\alpha)^{2}\gamma^{2}}\Big(\frac{1}{\gamma}+\frac{1-\gamma}{\varepsilon_{\rm dist}}\Big)\right).

∎

4.5
Resource state teleportation

Once we have prepared the resource state
|{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}\rangle\!\langle{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}|
(up to low error), the next step is to consume this resource state to enact a transformation on the encoded address qubits. Suppose the
n
-qubit encoded register on which we want to apply the operation
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g)$}}\smash[t]{V(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g)$}}\smash[t]{V(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(g)$}}\smash[t]{V(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g)$}}\smash[t]{V(g)}}}
is in an arbitrary pure state

\displaystyle|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}\rangle=\sum_{w\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\alpha_{w}|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$w$}}\smash[t]{w}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$w$}}\smash[t]{w}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$w$}}\smash[t]{w}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$w$}}\smash[t]{w}}}\rangle\,,\qquad\qquad\sum_{w}|\alpha_{w}|^{2}=1\,.

(95)

The teleportation procedure calls for applying a set of
n
disjoint logical CNOT gates, each controlled by one of the logical qubits in the register holding
|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}\rangle
and targeting one of the logical qubits in the resource state, and then measuring the resource state. This was depicted in
Eq.

10
, which is reproduced more compactly as

\scriptstyle n
\scriptstyle n

|{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}}\rangle\!\langle{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}}|

\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}|{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}}\rangle\!\langle{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}}|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}

|{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}\rangle\!\langle{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}|

A

m

(96)

On the output label of the circuit, we have used the notation
g^{\oplus m}
to represent the Boolean function defined for any
x
by the equation

\displaystyle g^{\oplus m}(x)=g(x\oplus m)\,.

(97)

To verify the circuit’s output and the assertions in
Section

2.3
, note that the CNOT gates enact the transformation

\displaystyle|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}\rangle|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}\rangle=\frac{1}{2^{n/2}}\sum_{w,z\in\{0,1\}^{n}}(-1)^{g(z)}\alpha_{w}|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$w$}}\smash[t]{w}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$w$}}\smash[t]{w}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$w$}}\smash[t]{w}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$w$}}\smash[t]{w}}}\rangle|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$z$}}\smash[t]{z}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$z$}}\smash[t]{z}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$z$}}\smash[t]{z}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$z$}}\smash[t]{z}}}\rangle\quad\longmapsto

\displaystyle\quad\frac{1}{2^{n/2}}\sum_{w,z\in\{0,1\}^{n}}(-1)^{g(z)}\alpha_{w}|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$w$}}\smash[t]{w}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$w$}}\smash[t]{w}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$w$}}\smash[t]{w}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$w$}}\smash[t]{w}}}\rangle|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$w\oplus z$}}\smash[t]{w\oplus z}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$w\oplus z$}}\smash[t]{w\oplus z}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$w\oplus z$}}\smash[t]{w\oplus z}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$w\oplus z$}}\smash[t]{w\oplus z}}}\rangle

(98)

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{2^{n/2}}\sum_{w,m\in\{0,1\}^{n}}(-1)^{g(w\oplus m)}\alpha_{w}|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$w$}}\smash[t]{w}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$w$}}\smash[t]{w}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$w$}}\smash[t]{w}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$w$}}\smash[t]{w}}}\rangle|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$m$}}\smash[t]{m}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$m$}}\smash[t]{m}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$m$}}\smash[t]{m}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$m$}}\smash[t]{m}}}\rangle\,.

(99)

A logical measurement is performed on the resource state register, obtaining outcome
m
. Regardless of the state
|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}\rangle
and the function
g
, the distribution over measurement outcomes
m
is the uniform distribution, and the post-measurement state on the first register is

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}\rangle=\frac{1}{2^{n/2}}\sum_{w\in\{0,1\}^{n}}(-1)^{g^{\oplus m}(w)}\alpha_{w}|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$w$}}\smash[t]{w}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$w$}}\smash[t]{w}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$w$}}\smash[t]{w}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$w$}}\smash[t]{w}}}\rangle=\frac{1}{2^{n/2}}\sum_{w\in\{0,1\}^{n}}(-1)^{g(w\oplus m)}\alpha_{w}|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$w$}}\smash[t]{w}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$w$}}\smash[t]{w}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$w$}}\smash[t]{w}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$w$}}\smash[t]{w}}}\rangle\,.

(100)

Examining the definition of
g^{\oplus m}
, we can see that it is defined by the same underlying table of data that defines
g
; however, the addresses to which the data items have been assigned are scrambled by adding (modulo 2) the random measurement outcome
m
to each address.
Nevertheless, we may conclude that the teleportation procedure successfully inserts information about
g
into the phases of the state
|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}\rangle
, albeit in an obfuscatedured way, due to the fact that the phase applied is
(-1)^{g(w\oplus m)}
rather than the desired
(-1)^{g(w)}
, where
m
is uniformly random.

We now wish to understand what happens in the teleportation step if we use the state
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}_{\rm dist}
(discussed in
Section

4.4
) as our resource state, which is an imperfect approximation to
|{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}\rangle\!\langle{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}|
. First, we define the ideal teleportation channel
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}
as the procedure that perfectly prepares
|{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}\rangle\!\langle{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}|
, applies the logical CNOTs, and measures the resource register (equivalently, applies a completely dephasing channel). This has the action

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}[\rho]=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\sum_{m\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{V}(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{V}(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{V}(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{V}(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{V}(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{V}(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{V}(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{V}(g^{\oplus m})}}}[\rho]\otimes|{m}\rangle\!\langle{m}|\,,

(101)

where we have used the channel definition
\mathcal{V}(h)[\rho]=V(h)\,\rho\,V(h)^{\dagger}
with the overlined version
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{V}(h)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{V}(h)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{V}(h)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{V}(h)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{V}(h)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{V}(h)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{V}(h)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{V}(h)}}}
denoting the logical version of the channel.

We may now argue that as long as the error of
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}_{\rm dist}
is low, relative to
|{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}\rangle\!\langle{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}|
, the channel enacted is close to
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}
.

Proposition 11

(Teleportation with approximate resource state)
.

Let
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}_{\rm appr}
be the channel realized by using the state
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}_{\rm dist}
in place of
|{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}\rangle\!\langle{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}|
in the teleportation protocol depicted in
Eq.

96
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\lVert\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}-\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}_{\rm appr}\rVert_{\diamond}\leq\frac{1}{2}\lVert|{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}\rangle\!\langle{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}|-\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}_{\rm dist}\rVert_{1}\,,

(102)

where
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\diamond}
indicates the diamond norm distance between channels.

Proof.

Define the quantum operation
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{C}$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{C}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{C}$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{C}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{C}$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{C}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{C}$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{C}}}}
as the operation on
2n
logical qubits enacted by the CNOT gates and measurements (i.e., completely dephasing channel) in
Eq.

96

Thus, for arbitrary
n
-qubit state
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\sigma$}}\smash[t]{\sigma}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\sigma$}}\smash[t]{\sigma}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\sigma$}}\smash[t]{\sigma}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\sigma$}}\smash[t]{\sigma}}}
in the codespace of the first register, we have

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}[\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\sigma$}}\smash[t]{\sigma}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\sigma$}}\smash[t]{\sigma}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\sigma$}}\smash[t]{\sigma}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\sigma$}}\smash[t]{\sigma}}}]

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{C}$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{C}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{C}$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{C}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{C}$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{C}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{C}$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{C}}}}\left[\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\sigma$}}\smash[t]{\sigma}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\sigma$}}\smash[t]{\sigma}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\sigma$}}\smash[t]{\sigma}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\sigma$}}\smash[t]{\sigma}}}\otimes|{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}\rangle\!\langle{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}|\right]

(103)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}_{\rm appr}[\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\sigma$}}\smash[t]{\sigma}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\sigma$}}\smash[t]{\sigma}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\sigma$}}\smash[t]{\sigma}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\sigma$}}\smash[t]{\sigma}}}]

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{C}$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{C}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{C}$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{C}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{C}$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{C}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{C}$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{C}}}}\left[\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\sigma$}}\smash[t]{\sigma}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\sigma$}}\smash[t]{\sigma}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\sigma$}}\smash[t]{\sigma}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\sigma$}}\smash[t]{\sigma}}}\otimes\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}_{\rm dist}\right]\,.

(104)

Now, introduce an environment register of
a
qubits, and consider an arbitrary state
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}
on
a+n
logical qubits (whose reduced density matrix after tracing out the environment is in the codespace of the
n
-logical-qubit code). Let
\mathcal{I}_{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.31883pt}{$E$}}\smash[t]{E}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.31883pt}{$E$}}\smash[t]{E}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.59442pt}{$E$}}\smash[t]{E}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.31883pt}{$E$}}\smash[t]{E}}}}
be the identity channel on the environment register. The diamond norm distance is defined as the supremum (over
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}
for all possible sizes
a
of the environment) in the trace distance between the action of the two channels

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\lVert\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}-\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}_{\rm appr}\rVert_{\diamond}

\displaystyle=\max_{a}\sup_{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.31883pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.31883pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.59442pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.31883pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}}\frac{1}{2}\lVert(\mathcal{I}_{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.31883pt}{$E$}}\smash[t]{E}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.31883pt}{$E$}}\smash[t]{E}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.59442pt}{$E$}}\smash[t]{E}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.31883pt}{$E$}}\smash[t]{E}}}}\otimes\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}})[\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}]-(\mathcal{I}_{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.31883pt}{$E$}}\smash[t]{E}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.31883pt}{$E$}}\smash[t]{E}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.59442pt}{$E$}}\smash[t]{E}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.31883pt}{$E$}}\smash[t]{E}}}}\otimes\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}_{\rm appr})[\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}]\rVert_{1}

(105)

\displaystyle=\max_{a}\sup_{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.31883pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.31883pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.59442pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.31883pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}}\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert(\mathcal{I}_{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.31883pt}{$E$}}\smash[t]{E}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.31883pt}{$E$}}\smash[t]{E}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.59442pt}{$E$}}\smash[t]{E}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.31883pt}{$E$}}\smash[t]{E}}}}\otimes\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{C}$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{C}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{C}$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{C}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{C}$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{C}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{C}$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{C}}}})\left[\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}\otimes\left(|{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}\rangle\!\langle{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}|-\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}_{\rm dist}\right)\right]\right\rVert_{1}

(106)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{a}\sup_{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.31883pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.31883pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.59442pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.31883pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}}\frac{1}{2}\lVert\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}\otimes\left(|{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}\rangle\!\langle{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}|-\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}_{\rm dist}\right)\rVert_{1}

(107)

\displaystyle=\max_{a}\sup_{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.31883pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.31883pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.59442pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.31883pt}{$\rho$}}\smash[t]{\rho}}}}\frac{1}{2}\lVert|{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}\rangle\!\langle{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}|-\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}_{\rm dist}\rVert_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\lVert|{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}\rangle\!\langle{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}|-\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}_{\rm dist}\rVert_{1}\,,

(108)

where the inequality follows from monotonicity of the trace distance under quantum channels. This completes the proof.
∎

4.6
Adaptive correction and classical update rule

Recall that the goal is to implement the diagonal logical QRAM unitary
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}
, given a data table (Boolean function)
f
. The previous section established that for any function
g
, the teleportation channel
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}
receives a uniformly random measurement outcome
m
, and then, conditioned on
m
, enacts the unitary transformation
|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}\rangle\mapsto\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\alpha$}}\smash[t]{\alpha}}}\rangle
.

Our protocol repeats the teleportation process over a sequence of
n
rounds. In each round, the function
g
will be different, and the measurement outcome
m
will be sampled independently and uniformly at random. The value of
g
used in round
j
will be denoted
g^{(j)}
, and the value of
m
denoted by
m^{(j)}
.

We begin in round 1 setting
g=g^{(1)}=f
, receiving measurement outcome
m=m^{(1)}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, and enacting logical unitary
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}
. The key observation is that
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}^{2}=\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathbb{I}$}}\smash[t]{\mathbb{I}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathbb{I}$}}\smash[t]{\mathbb{I}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{I}$}}\smash[t]{\mathbb{I}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathbb{I}$}}\smash[t]{\mathbb{I}}}}
, the logical identity operation, and hence

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g)$}}\smash[t]{V(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g)$}}\smash[t]{V(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(g)$}}\smash[t]{V(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g)$}}\smash[t]{V(g)}}}=\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g)$}}\smash[t]{V(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g)$}}\smash[t]{V(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(g)$}}\smash[t]{V(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g)$}}\smash[t]{V(g)}}}\;\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}\;\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}=\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g\oplus g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g\oplus g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g\oplus g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g\oplus g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(g\oplus g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g\oplus g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g\oplus g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g\oplus g^{\oplus m})}}}\;\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}\,,

(109)

where we have invoked the composition rule
V(h)V(h^{\prime})=V(h\oplus h^{\prime})
. Thus, given that we have already implemented
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g^{\oplus m})}}}
, we must now implement the
correction unitary

\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g\oplus g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g\oplus g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g\oplus g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g\oplus g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(g\oplus g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g\oplus g^{\oplus m})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g\oplus g^{\oplus m})$}}\smash[t]{V(g\oplus g^{\oplus m})}}}
, which is also a member of the family of diagonal (logical) QRAM operators. We thus compute a new Boolean function by the
classical update rule

\mathrm{UR}
, which takes as input a data table
g
and a bit string
m\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and outputs a new data table

\displaystyle\mathrm{UR}(g,m)=g\oplus g^{\oplus m}\,,

(110)

which is depicted as a circuit as

\scriptstyle n
\scriptstyle 2^{n}
\scriptstyle 2^{n}

m

g

\rm UR

\rm UR

g\oplus g^{\oplus m}

(111)

and we update
g\leftarrow g^{(2)}=\mathrm{UR}(g^{(1)},m^{(1)})
to use for round 2. Consequently, the correction unitary is now equal to
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g)$}}\smash[t]{V(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g)$}}\smash[t]{V(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(g)$}}\smash[t]{V(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g)$}}\smash[t]{V(g)}}}
and the goal of round
2
is again simply to implement the unitary
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g)$}}\smash[t]{V(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g)$}}\smash[t]{V(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(g)$}}\smash[t]{V(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(g)$}}\smash[t]{V(g)}}}
, just as it was in round 1. As before, we receive a new measurement outcome
m=m^{(2)}
, we compute
g^{(3)}=\mathrm{UR}(g^{(2)},m^{(2)})
, and we update
g\leftarrow g^{(3)}
for round 3.

We then iterate this process a number of times. We note that, if after applying the update rule we ever obtain
g=\mathbf{0}
, the zero function, then we may terminate the procedure, because the correction unitary will be
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(\mathbf{0})$}}\smash[t]{V(\mathbf{0})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(\mathbf{0})$}}\smash[t]{V(\mathbf{0})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(\mathbf{0})$}}\smash[t]{V(\mathbf{0})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(\mathbf{0})$}}\smash[t]{V(\mathbf{0})}}}=\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathbb{I}$}}\smash[t]{\mathbb{I}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathbb{I}$}}\smash[t]{\mathbb{I}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{I}$}}\smash[t]{\mathbb{I}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathbb{I}$}}\smash[t]{\mathbb{I}}}}
at the next round. Moreover,
g=\mathbf{1}
(the constant function that outputs 1 on all inputs), then the correction unitary is
-\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathbb{I}$}}\smash[t]{\mathbb{I}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathbb{I}$}}\smash[t]{\mathbb{I}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{I}$}}\smash[t]{\mathbb{I}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathbb{I}$}}\smash[t]{\mathbb{I}}}}
, which is equivalent to
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathbb{I}$}}\smash[t]{\mathbb{I}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathbb{I}$}}\smash[t]{\mathbb{I}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{I}$}}\smash[t]{\mathbb{I}}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathbb{I}$}}\smash[t]{\mathbb{I}}}}
up to an unphysical global sign. We claim that after at most
n
rounds, we will be certain to obtain
g\in\{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{1}\}
, based on the following proposition.

Proposition 12
.

Let
g
be an
n
-bit Boolean function. Define
\deg(g)
to be the degree of
g
when it is expanded as a polynomial of its input bits over the field
\mathbb{F}_{2}
. Suppose that
\deg(g)=d
. Let
m\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, and let
h=\mathrm{UR}(g,m)
, as defined in
Eq.

110
. Then, we have

\deg(h)\leq d-1\,.

(112)

Proof.

This is a consequence of the reasoning in
Appendix

D
, specifically the cancellation in
Eq.

242
and the reasoning underneath.
∎

The proposition establishes that each application of the update rule
g\leftarrow\mathrm{UR}(g,m)
decreases the degree of
g
by at least 1. Recall that in round
1
, we have
\deg(g)=\deg(f)\leq n
, simply by virtue of the fact that
f
is an
n
-bit function. Thus, we may assert that in round
j
we have
g=g^{(j)}
and

\displaystyle\deg(g^{(j)})\leq n+1-j\,.

(113)

In particular, after applying the update rule in round
n
with
g=g^{(n)}
and
m=m^{(n)}
, we are guaranteed to obtain
h=\mathrm{UR}(g,m)
, which leads to a degree
\deg(h)=0
. If the degree of
h
is 0, this implies that either
h=\mathbf{0}
or
h=\mathbf{1}
.

4.7
Total complexity of protocol

We have now explained the action of each component of the protocol, and may we state its overall complexity.

Theorem 2

(Main result)
.

Let
f
be an arbitrary dataset (
n
-bit Boolean function) for which we wish to implement the fault-tolerant diagonal QRAM unitary
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}
of
Eq.

1
, and let
\varepsilon
be an error parameter. Suppose that we have access to a noisy physical QRAM device subject to dataset-independent noise (
Definition

1
) which on input
g
produces state
\widetilde{\vphantom{\raisebox{1.43109pt}{:}}\smash[t]{\psi}}(g)
on
n
physical qubits achieving fidelity
\langle\Psi(g)|\widetilde{\vphantom{\raisebox{1.43109pt}{:}}\smash[t]{\psi}}(g)|\Psi(g)\rangle\geq F
for all
g
. Suppose further that we have the capability to reload the QRAM device with a new dataset, and that we have the capability to move the

n
-qubit output state to a fault-tolerant quantum processor subject to circuit-level stochastic noise (
Definition

2
) with error rate
p
. If
p
is below a constant threshold
p_{0}
determined by the QEC code family, and separately if
pn^{2}
is below a different constant threshold related to the fault-tolerant encoding procedure, then there exists an adaptive distillation–teleportation procedure that implements a quantum channel
\mathcal{P}_{\rm DT}
for which

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\lVert\mathcal{P}_{\rm DT}-\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}[\cdot]\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.40881pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}^{\dagger}\rVert_{\diamond}\leq\varepsilon\,.

(114)

The protocol uses (in expectation over internal randomness)
Q
queries to the noisy physical QRAM device,
Q
applications of the encoding process
\mathcal{E}_{\rm FT}
(
Corollary

1
), and
Q^{\prime}
additional fault-tolerant operations (controlled-SWAP, Hadamard, CNOT, single-qubit logical state preparations, and single-qubit logical measurements), where

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{n(1-F)}{F^{2}}\left(\frac{n}{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{F}\right)\right)

(115)

\displaystyle Q^{\prime}

\displaystyle=O\left(n^{2}Q\right)\,.

(116)

Additionally, the protocol applies the classical update rule (
Eq.

110
) at most
n
times, and the classical partial Clifford twirling operation
g\mapsto g_{C}
(
Eq.

35
)
Q
times, each time on a data table of size
2^{n}
classical bits.

Proof.

The protocol is depicted as a quantum circuit in
Fig.

2
. We begin with correctness. In each of the
n
rounds indexed by
j=1,\ldots,n
, it implements a channel
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g^{(j)})$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g^{(j)})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g^{(j)})$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g^{(j)})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g^{(j)})$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g^{(j)})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g^{(j)})$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g^{(j)})}}}
. As discussed in
Section

4.6
, if all resource states are prepared perfectly, the procedure is guaranteed to implement the unitary
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}
, up to a global sign which does not impact the channel
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}[\cdot]\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$V(f)$}}\smash[t]{V(f)}}}^{\dagger}
.

However, the teleportation channel
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g^{(j)})$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g^{(j)})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g^{(j)})$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g^{(j)})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g^{(j)})$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g^{(j)})}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g^{(j)})$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g^{(j)})}}}
is not implemented perfectly by the protocol. To ensure the overall diamond norm error is
\varepsilon
, it suffices to choose parameters such that
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\mathcal{T}(g)$}}\smash[t]{\mathcal{T}(g)}}}
is implemented up to
\varepsilon/n
diamond distance for all
g
, since the errors from each of the
n
channel applications add linearly in the worst case, when performed in succession. By
Proposition

11
, it suffices to distill resource states
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}_{\rm dist}
that satisfy

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\lVert|{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}\rangle\!\langle{\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}}|-\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}_{\rm dist}\rVert_{1}\leq\varepsilon_{\rm dist}

(117)

with distillation error
\varepsilon_{\rm dist}=\varepsilon/n
. Meanwhile, by
Proposition

6
, this is accomplished by the distillation protocol using
O(\frac{1-F_{\rm min}}{F_{\rm min}^{2}}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{\rm dist}}+\frac{1}{F_{\rm min}}))
copies of the input states
\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}_{\rm twirl}
, defined in
Eq.

43
, provided that for all
g

\langle\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\phi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\phi(g)}}}|\mathchoice{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}{\overline{\vphantom{\raisebox{-0.39993pt}{$\Psi(g)$}}\smash[t]{\Psi(g)}}}\rangle\geq F_{\rm min}
for some
F_{\rm min}
.
We are guaranteed from
Corollary

1
that
F_{\rm min}\geq(1-O(np)-O(n\sqrt{p}))F
, which can be replaced by
\Omega(F)
as long as
pn^{2}
is below a certain constant. The number of gates required by distillation is a factor of
O(n)
larger than the number of copies.

Since there are
n
rounds, we require
n
calls to the distillation procedure. Thus, the total number of queries to the noisy QRAM device and the
\mathrm{poly}(n)
-cost encoding procedure
\mathcal{E}_{\rm FT}
is

\displaystyle Q=O\left(\frac{n(1-F)}{F^{2}}\left(\frac{n}{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{F}\right)\right)

(118)

The total fault-tolerant gate complexity from distillation is
O(nQ)
. The teleportation procedure also requires
n
fault-tolerant CNOT gates in each of the
n
rounds. Finally, the Clifford twirling step requires the application of
O(n^{2})
fault-tolerant Clifford gates for each of the
Q
copies. In total, these Clifford gates dominate the fault-tolerant gate count, which is
Q^{\prime}=O(n^{2}Q)
.
Each round requires only one call to the update rule, and each of the
Q
copies requires classically applying a partial Clifford update
g\mapsto g_{C}
. This completes the proof.
∎

5
Complexity of the classical update rule

The classical update rule calls for updating a data table
g
to the data table
h=\mathrm{UR}(g,m)=g\oplus g^{\oplus m}
, for a certain fixed measurement outcome
m\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, as in
Eq.

110
. That is, the entry at address
x
in the data table should be updated from
g(x)
to
g(x)\oplus g(x\oplus m)
. In this section, we analyze the complexity of this transformation under several different frameworks. The guiding question is to understand the ways in which the classical update rule is a more complex operation than a RAM query.

We note that the partial Clifford twirling step also requires substantial classical computation to randomly transform the dataset. We do not specifically analyze this step here, because we view it as less fundamental to our protocol. For example, if the physical QRAM device and encoding step were error free (or if they have errors but the principal eigenvalue of the resulting state is correct), then partial Clifford twirling is not necessary, but the need for the update rule remains.

5.1
Classical circuit complexity

The update rule takes
2^{n}+n
bits as input and produces
2^{n}
bits as output, as in
Eq.

111
. It is straightforward to see that a classical circuit built from elementary gates (NOT, AND, NAND, etc.) would require
\Omega(2^{n})
gates to implement the update rule. Observe that for any fixed nonzero value
m\neq 0^{n}
, we have for every
x

\displaystyle h(x)=h(x\oplus m)=g(x)\oplus g(x\oplus m)

(119)

That is, the
2^{n}
addresses are partitioned into pairs
\{x,x\oplus m\}
, where
h
takes the same value on both elements of the pair, and its value is equal to the parity of the input bits at locations
x
and
x\oplus m
. Each of these
2^{n-1}
parity calculations is independent and requires at least one elementary gate.

5.2
Complexity in a classical RAM model

The need for
\Omega(2^{n})
circuit complexity does not alone entail that the update rule is an expensive classical calculation. After all, the standard RAM operation also has
\Omega(2^{n})
circuit complexity, but it is commonplace to consider a model of classical computation where RAM has unit cost.

However, the single-bit RAM operation takes as input
n
bits (an address) and returns just 1 bit, so it appears to be a much simpler operation than the classical update rule. Since (i) the output of the update rule has
2^{n}
bits, (ii) the output depends on all
2^{n}
input bits
g(x)
, and (iii) each RAM query can access only 1 of the bits, we conclude that, in a model where the input data
g
can only be accessed via RAM queries, implementing the update rule requires
\Omega(2^{n})
RAM queries.

5.3
Classical circuit depth in an all-to-all model

The structured nature of the update rule suggests it may still be amenable to some degree of parallelization. Ideally, one could design a specialized shallow circuit that directly implements the update, rather than relying on RAM. Indeed, if one imposes no restrictions on spatial layout of the
2^{n}+n
input bits and
2^{n}
output bits (i.e., one allows all-to-all gates), then one can perform the update rule with a classical circuit of depth
O(n)
comprised of elementary gates each acting on only
O(1)
bits.

We provide one possible way to accomplish this. The construction requires
O(n2^{n})
ancilla bits and has the following steps; we provide an
n=3
example to assist with understanding each step in
Fig.

5
(and in the description of each step, we reference the colors in that figure for clarity of explanation). Let
e_{i}
refer to the length-
n
bit string with a 1 in the
i
-th position and 0 in the other
n-1
positions.

1.

For each
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
in parallel, the (blue) bit holding
g(x)
is copied into a (yellow) ancilla bit, accomplished in depth 1.

2.

For each
i=1,\ldots,n
in parallel, the (green) input bit holding
m_{i}
is copied into
2^{n-1}-1
(gray) ancilla bits, accomplished in depth
n-1
(using a tree-like approach, the number of copies of
m_{i}
can be doubled with each additional circuit layer).

3.

For each
i=1,\ldots,n
in series, and for each of the
2^{n-1}
address pairs
\{x,x\oplus e_{i}\}
in parallel, if
m_{i}=1
then the (yellow) ancilla bit which held the copy of
g(x)
at the beginning of this step is swapped with the (yellow) ancilla bit which held the copy of
g(x\oplus e_{i})
at the beginning of this step. Each value of
i
requires depth 1: the availability of
2^{n-1}
(green and gray) copies of the
m_{i}
bit created in step 2 allows parallelization of the
m_{i}
-controlled
\{x,x\oplus e_{i}\}
swaps. Thus, the overall depth of this step is
n
. Over the course of the
n
steps, the (yellow) ancilla bit that originally held
g(x)
before this step undergoes the transformations

g(x)\overset{i=1}{\mapsto}g(x\oplus m_{1}e_{1})\overset{i=2}{\mapsto}g(x\oplus m_{1}e_{1}\oplus m_{2}e_{2})\overset{i=3}{\mapsto}\cdots\overset{i=n}{\mapsto}g(x\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}e_{i})=g(x\oplus m)\,,

(120)

that is, for each
x
, the (yellow) ancilla bit originally holding
g(x)
now holds
g(x\oplus m)
.

4.

For each
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
in parallel, the (yellow) ancilla bit that originally held the copy of
g(x)
after step 1 (which now holds
g(x\oplus m)
) is added modulo 2 into the (blue) bit holding
g(x)
, incurring depth
1
.

5.

The original
2^{n}
(blue) input bits are taken to be the
2^{n}
output bits; the ancilla bits are discarded.

The registers (blue) holding the original bits
g(x)
have now been updated to
h(x)=g(x)\oplus g(x\oplus m)
, which is the desired output of the update rule.

Figure 5
:
Step-by-step action of the
O(n)
-depth classical circuit that implements the update rule
g\mapsto h=g\oplus g^{\oplus m}
, for a particular
n=3
example input with
m=(1,0,1)
. Each box stores one bit of information, and each step modifies some subset of these bits with parallelized layers of local gates (note that step 2 requires
n-1
layers to create all
2^{n-1}-1
copies of each
m_{i}
). The inputs to the update rule are the
n
bits of
m
(green) and the
2^{n}
bits in the classical data table storing
g
(blue). The circuit utilizes
2^{n}+n(2^{n-1}-1)
ancilla bits (gray and yellow).

5.4
Classical circuit depth in a spatially local model

If the input and output bits of the shallow circuit are embedded into
d
spatial dimensions, then the
O(n)
-depth circuit above requires spatially nonlocal gates. For example, we may recognize the action of step 3 as a re-arrangement of the
2^{n}
(yellow) ancilla bits by traversing edges of the
n
-dimensional Boolean hypercube. In
d=n
spatial dimensions, this could be done using spatially local gates, and each (yellow) classical bit need only interact with
O(n)
of the
2^{n}
other (yellow) bits (its neighbors on the hypercube). Unfortunately, real classical circuits must be embedded into
d\leq 3
spatial dimensions. In this case, step 3 cannot be performed exclusively with spatially local gates for more than
d
of the values of
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

In fact, we can show that if gates are local in
d
spatial dimensions, then the depth required is at least
\Omega(2^{n/d})
. This follows from the fact that without knowing the value of
m
, the circuit must be prepared to connect the bit at address
x
to all
2^{n}-1
of the other bits; for any pair
x,y\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, if
m=x\oplus y
, then the circuit must be able to compute the parity of
g(x)
and
g(y)
. If the bits storing
g(x)
and
g(y)
live on opposite sides of the
d
-dimensional array, computing this parity will require a classical circuit with depth at least
\Omega(2^{n/d})
. Ultimately, this essentially amounts to a speed of light–type restriction, where depth is restricted due to the fact that information can only move so quickly through space. This kind of argument could also be used to show that classical RAM requires a circuit of depth
\Omega(2^{n/d})
in a local model, in
d
spatial dimensions, so it does not represent a fundamental limitation that is unique to QRAM.

5.5
Wire density

While speed of light–type restrictions can be relevant for RAM at large scales, they are not a factor in practice at small or intermediate scales. If one ignores the speed of light, one can simply build long wires into the circuit and enable all-to-all connectivity. These long wires should not be thought of as completely free, however. For example, electronic ciruits typically dissipate energy and lead to heating in proportion to their total wire length. The need to cool electronic chips is a key limitation in practical computer systems. Thus, a cost metric worth considering
[
8
]
is the wire density of the circuit, which we define as the total wire length divided by the total spacetime of the circuit, where the spacetime is defined as the number of bits the circuit uses (henceforth referred to as the circuit width) multiplied by the circuit depth.

Classical circuits for RAM can have depth
O(n)
, width
O(2^{n})
, and total wire length
O(n2^{n})

[
8
]
, even when embedded in one spatial dimension. Thus, the wire density is a constant with respect to
n
, suggesting the circuit can be scaled without causing heating issues.

We now examine the circuit for the update rule described in
Section

5.3
. It has depth
O(n)
and width
O(n2^{n})
. Steps 1 and 2 perform copying of the bits and contribute wire length
O(n2^{n})
. If the circuit is embedded in
n
spatial dimensions, then step 3 can also be accomplished with total wire length
O(n2^{n})
, as each gate is local and has
O(1)
wire length. However, in
d\leq 3
spatial dimensions, the wire length is asymptotically larger. To implement step 3, the (yellow) ancilla bits storing the copy of
g(x)
must be connected to the (yellow) ancilla bits initially storing the copies of
g(x\oplus e_{1})
,
g(x\oplus e_{2})
, …,
g(x\oplus e_{n})
—essentially, an embedding of the
n
-dimensional Boolean hypercube into
d
dimensions. Consequently, the total wire length of gates acting on each (yellow) ancilla bit will be at least
\Omega(2^{n/d})
. Since there are
2^{n}
(yellow) ancilla bits, the overall wire length of the circuit is at least
\Omega(2^{n(1+1/d)})
and thus the wire density grows with
n
as
\Omega(2^{n/d}/\mathrm{poly}(n))
, a fundamentally different outcome than the case of classical RAM.

5.6
Relation to matrix-vector multiplication and the Walsh–Hadamard transform

matrix-vector multiplication for
2^{n}\times 2^{n}
matrices is an operation with
2^{n}
inputs (the entries of the input vector) and
2^{n}
outputs (the entries of the output vector). This feature is similar to the update rule, although the inputs and outputs for matrix multipliation would typically each be multiple bits (e.g., an integer or floating point number), rather than just a single bit. Moreover, the analysis of Ref.
[
8
]
demonstrated how classical sparse matrix-vector multiplication requires a growing wire density, consistent with the observation above for the update rule.

Here, we will argue that the update rule is in a certain sense equivalent to a sparse matrix-vector multiplication, and specifically it is equivalent to the Walsh–Hadamard (WH) transform up to factors of
\mathrm{poly}(n)
. This equivalence holds under a parallel model of computation. Namely, we assume that we have
2^{n}
classical co-processors, each with
\mathrm{poly}(n)
-size local memory, which may perform local arithmetic on their memory in parallel, but cannot communicate with one another, except through joint application of the update rule or through joint application of a sparse matrix-vector multiplication. When jointly applying the update rule, each of the
2^{n}
processors supplies one entry
g(x)
and receives the output
h(x)=g(x)\oplus g(x\oplus m)
for a fixed global
m
. When jointly applying sparse matrix-vector multiplication, each processor provides one of the
2^{n}
entries of the input vector and receives one of the
2^{n}
entries of the output vector. The remainder of this subsection aims to justify this claim of equivalence.

5.6.1
The (fast) Walsh–Hadamard transform

The WH transform is the multiplication of a length-
2^{n}
vector by a
2^{n}\times 2^{n}
matrix denoted by
H
, where the matrix element associated with the transition from
n
-bit input address
y
to
n
-bit output address
x
is given by

\displaystyle H_{xy}=\frac{1}{2^{n/2}}(-1)^{x\cdot y}

(121)

We can see that
H_{xy}
is a dense matrix—all of its entries are nonzero—however, it can be shown that it is the product of
n
sparse matrices. Specifically, let
H^{(i)}
be defined as
9
9

9

We note that the matrix
H^{(i)}
is proportional (by a factor
\sqrt{2}
) to the transformation applied to the amplitudes of a quantum state when a single-qubit Hadamard gate is applied to the
i
-th qubit, and identity is applied to the other
n-1
qubits. The full WH transform is the product of the
H^{(i)}
matrices, that is, the Hadamard gate on all
n
qubits.

\displaystyle H^{(i)}_{xy}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }x=y\oplus e_{i}\\
(-1)^{x_{i}y_{i}}&\text{if }x=y\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(122)

Then, it holds that

\displaystyle H=\frac{1}{2^{n/2}}H^{(n)}H^{(n-1)}\cdots H^{(2)}H^{(1)}\,,

(123)

This fact can be verified by noting that the nonzero offdiagonal entries of
H^{(i)}
are matrix elements
H^{(i)}_{uv}
where
u
and
v
differ only on the
i
-th bit. Thus, any offdiagonal transition element
H_{xy}
can only be obtained in the product
H^{(n)}\cdots H^{(1)}
by choosing the corresponding offdiagonal entry of
H^{(i)}
, (equal to 1) whenever
x_{i}\neq y_{i}
differ, and the diagonal entry
(-1)^{x_{i}y_{i}}
of
H^{(i)}
whenever
x_{i}=y_{i}
. This gives precisely the quantity
(-1)^{\sum_{i}x_{i}y_{i}}=(-1)^{x\cdot y}
.

Matrix multiplication by
H^{(i)}
requires only
O(2^{n})
arithmetic operations, since each row of
H^{(i)}
has only 2 nonzero entries. The fast WH transform utilizes this decomposition to implement multiplication by
H
in classical time
\mathrm{poly}(n)2^{n}
, much smaller than the
\Omega(2^{2n})
time required to multiply general dense matrices that do not have this kind of decomposition.

5.6.2
Reduction from update rule to Walsh–Hadamard transform

Now, we show how the update rule can be accomplished with two applications of the WH transform and parallel local arithmetic. Let
\mathbf{g}
denote the length-
2^{n}
vector with entry
g(x)
at index
x
, and let
\mathbf{h}
denote the length-
2^{n}
vector with entry
g(x)+g(x\oplus m)
at entry
x
. Note here that we are using normal addition
+
, rather than modular addition
\oplus
, and we allow
\mathbf{h}
to take integer values in
\{0,1,2\}
. We have that the
x
-th entry of the matrix-vector product
H\mathbf{h}
is

\displaystyle(H\mathbf{h})_{x}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{n/2}}\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}^{n}}(-1)^{x\cdot y}(g(y)+g(y\oplus m))

(124)

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{1}{2^{n/2}}\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}^{n}}(-1)^{x\cdot y}g(y)\right]+(-1)^{m\cdot x}\left[\frac{1}{2^{n/2}}\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}^{n}}(-1)^{x\cdot(y\oplus m)}g(y\oplus m)\right]

(125)

\displaystyle=(H\mathbf{g})_{x}+(-1)^{m\cdot x}(H\mathbf{g})_{x}

(126)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}2(H\mathbf{g})_{x}&\text{if }m\cdot x=0\\
0&\text{if }m\cdot x=1\end{cases}

(127)

That is, the WH transform
H\mathbf{h}
of the update rule output
\mathbf{h}
can be easily computed from the WH transform
H\mathbf{g}
of the update rule input
\mathbf{g}
.

Let
M^{(m)}
be the diagonal matrix for which
M^{(m)}_{xx}=2
if
m\cdot x=0
and
M^{(m)}_{xx}=0
if
m\cdot x=1
. Then, we may use the equation above to say that
H\mathbf{h}=MH\mathbf{g}
. Since
H^{2}=\mathbb{I}
, we then have

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}=HH\mathbf{h}=HM^{(m)}H\mathbf{g}

(128)

This gives a straightforward way to transform
\mathbf{g}\mapsto\mathbf{h}
in the model where
2^{n}
classical co-processors can perform local arithmetic in parallel or jointly perform the WH transform. First, we assume that
2^{n}
processors each locally store the bit
g(x)
for one address
x
, and an
n
-bit copy of
m
(note that a copy of
m
could be pre-distributed to each of the
2^{n}
processors via a tree-like circuit of constant wire density, as in step 2 of
Section

5.3
). Then,
\mathbf{g}\mapsto\mathbf{h}
is accomplished by applying the WH transform, applying the diagonal transformation
M^{(m)}
, and finally applying the WH transform again. The
x
-th entry of the diagonal matrix
M^{(m)}
can be computed in
O(1)
depth locally by the
x
-th processor acting on its internal memory. We recall that
\mathbf{h}
may have entries in
\{0,1,2\}
; to recover binary entries, we simply take every entry modulo 2 via parallel local arithmetic, which does not discard any important information, owing to the fact that
(-1)^{g(x)+g(x\oplus m)}=(-1)^{g(x)\oplus g(x\oplus m)}
.

The conclusion is that
O(1)
applications of the WH transform, along with parallel local arithmetic, is sufficient to implement the classical update rule, or in other words, the classical update rule is no harder than the WH transform.

5.6.3
Reduction from WH transform to update rule

Now, we show the converse: that the WH transform can be implemented using
\mathrm{poly}(n)
applications of the update rule along with parallel local arithmetic. Specifically, we aim to implement the matrix transformation
H^{(i)}
. Suppose we are given a vector
\mathbf{g}
of integers each represented by at most
n
bits.
10
10

10

The assumption of integer entries is without loss of generality. If the entries are not integers but rather non-integer numbers expressed in binary with a finite number of bits of precision, then we can always multiply by a power of 2 so that all the entries are integers, and divide by that power of 2 after performing the calculation.
Let
g(x)
denote the integer corresponding to the entry of
\mathbf{g}
at address
x
, which is stored in the local memory of one of the
2^{n}
processors. Let
g_{j}(x)
denote the
j
-th bit of the integer
g(x)
. We perform the following steps

1.

For each address
x
, the co-processor storing
g(x)
makes a copy of
g(x)
in its local memory.

2.

We fix global
n
-bit string
m=e_{i}
(known by all processors) and for each
j
, the
2^{n}
co-procesoors jointly apply the update rule
\mathrm{UR}(g_{j},e_{i})
onto the set of
2^{n}
bits
g_{j}(x)
(i.e., bit-wise application of update rule). For each
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, and each
j
, the co-processor at address
x
now has in its memory the value
g_{j}(x)
and the value
g_{j}(x)\oplus g_{j}(x\oplus e_{i})
.

3.

For each
x
and each
j
, the co-processor at address
x
performs local arithmetic to add (modulo 2) the local register holding
g_{j}(x)
into the local register holding
g_{j}(x)\oplus g_{j}(x\oplus e_{i})
so that the two registers now hold
g_{j}(x)
and
g_{j}(x\oplus e_{i})
. Effectively, this step and the previous step have together performed a swap
g(x)\leftrightarrow g(x\oplus e_{i})
between the various co-processors.

4.

For each address
x
, the co-processor at address
x
takes the local register holding integer
g(x)
and flips it to
-g(x)
only if
x_{i}=1
.

5.

Finally, for each address
x
, the co-processor at address
x
adds the local register holding
(-1)^{x_{i}}g(x)
into the local register holding
g(x\oplus e_{i})
, such that the latter register now holds
g(x\oplus e_{i})+(-1)^{x_{i}}g(x)
.

The latter register in the local memory of co-processor
x
now holds precisely the value
(H^{(i)}\mathbf{g})_{x}
, indicating that the co-processors have successfully managed to apply one step of the fast WH transform. Accomplishing this required one application of the update rule for each bit of the entries of the vector. Assuming the integer entries have at most
\mathrm{poly}(n)
bits, this means
H^{(i)}
can be accomplished with
\mathrm{poly}(n)
applications of the update rule, and local arithmetic, specifically, copying (step 1), bit-wise addition mod 2 (step 3), negation (step 4), and integer addition (step 5).

Since
H
is the product of
n
matrices
H^{(i)}
up to a proportionality constant, we conclude that
\mathrm{poly}(n)
applications of the update rule are sufficient to implement the WH transform in this model of computation; in other words, the WH transform is no harder than the update rule, up to a factor of
\mathrm{poly}(n)
.

5.6.4
Performing general sparse matrix-vector multiplication using the update rule

Multiplication by
H^{(i)}
is in some sense easier than multiplication by an arbitrary sparse matrix, since (i) the location of the nonzero entries is highly structured and (ii) all entries are
\pm 1
, avoiding the need for any integer or floating-point multiplications. The only arithmetic required is addition and subtraction. Here we discuss how the update rule can also be used for arbitrary sparse matrix-vector multiplications.

Lemma A.3 of Ref.
[
8
]
examines performing general sparse matrix-vector multiplication using parallel classical co-processors, each capable of local addition and multiplication. If the parallel processors can communicate with each other via links that form a sorting network, then the sparse matrix-vector multiplication can be accomplished in roughly the time required to perform a sort using the sorting network. As shown in steps 2 and 3 of the procedure in
Section

5.6.3
, the update rule and parallel bitwise xor together allow a swap of data at location
x
and location
x\oplus e_{i}
for all
x
in parallel. By copying the data before performing the swap, and then choosing whether to discard the original copy or the swapped copy, one can use the update rule to swap in parallel any
subset
of the location pairs
(x,x\oplus e_{i})
. Thus, the ability to perform the update rule enables access to parallel swaps along a Boolean hypercube connectivity in
n
dimensions, a model for which it is known that sorting can be completed in
\mathrm{poly}(n)
time
[
107
,
108
]
.
Together with Ref.
[
8
, Lemma A.3]
